description: "How the ZX Spectrum Next's beeper works: the two sound bits of port $FE, EAR and MIC, and their levels in the mixer, a note made by a timing loop and why the CPU's speed and contention change its pitch, two voices from one bit with short pulses, saving to tape through the MIC socket, the input stage that cleans up a tape's signal before port $FE bit 6 reads it, the issue 2 keyboard, the internal speaker's beeper-only mode, and how Klive emulates all of it."

import { Callout } from 'nextra/components'

# The Beeper: EAR, MIC and the Tape

Manic Miner plays a two-voice tune on a machine whose sound is a single bit. The same bit's
neighbour tells the tape recorder what to save, and the same port reads back what the tape plays.
So how does one bit make music, and how do music and data share one wire without tripping over each
other?

The beeper is the oldest sound on the Next, and still the one every Spectrum program knows. A 48K
game's music and effects, the click of a key in BASIC, the screech of a program loading, and every
program ever saved to cassette all go through it. Your Next runs those programs unchanged, so it has to
reproduce the beeper exactly: the levels, the timing, and the small details of what a program reads
back, which some old programs depend on more than their authors would like to admit.

Read [The Big Picture: Four Sources, One Mixer](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-audio/overview/) first: it
introduces the four kinds of sound source, and this is the first of them. If square waves and sound from
a single bit are new to you, the background chapter
[How a Computer Makes Sound](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/computer-sound/) explains them, and
[ZX Spectrum: How the Screen Is Drawn](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/spectrum-display/)
explains port `$FE`, which the beeper shares with the border, and contention. Where the beeper's
levels go after the mixer is the subject of
[The Mixer: One Sum, Three Outputs](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-audio/mixer/).

## The road ahead

We start with the port: the two bits of port `$FE` that make sound, why there are two of them, and how
loud each one is in the mixer. Then you play a note with a timing loop, and see why the CPU's speed and
contention change its pitch, the second one in a rather surprising way. The key idea comes next: how short
pulses let a single bit play two voices at once, and what that trick costs. After that, the tape: saving
through the MIC socket, then loading through the EAR socket, where an input stage cleans up the tape's
signal before your program reads it, and where the issue 2 keyboard still lurks. We end with the internal
speaker, which can keep the beeper all to itself, and with how Klive does all of this.

<Callout type="info">
The CPU runs at **3.5 MHz** with contention **off** unless a section says otherwise; times in T-states are
CPU clock cycles at that speed. Levels are in the **units of the Next's mixer**, whose sum runs from 0
to 8,191 per side. The waveforms are captured from Klive's emulated Next, from programs checked to do
what the text says, at 48,000 samples a second; they show Klive's mix, which stands for the sound after
the audio jack's capacitor and so moves around zero.
</Callout>

## Two bits of sound

The beeper lives in **port `$FE`**, the ULA's port. Any write to an even port address (address bit 0 is 0)
writes it, and three of its fields matter:

<figure>
{/* fig:bits */}
<svg viewBox="0 0 700 206" role="img" aria-label="Port $FE as two strips of eight bits, bit 7 on the left. Written: bits 7 to 5 are ignored, bit 4 is EAR, the beeper, bit 3 is MIC, the tape output, and bits 2 to 0 are the border colour. Read: bit 7 reads 1, bit 6 is the tape input, bit 5 reads 1, and bits 4 to 0 are the keyboard, one bit per key of the selected rows, 0 for a key held. Any even port address is port $FE, and bit 6 also reads the EAR and MIC bits back." style={{ width: "100%", height: "auto" }}>

</svg>
{/* /fig:bits */}
<figcaption>Port $FE as a program writes it and reads it, bit 7 on the left. Blue bits are the beeper's and the tape's; grey bits do other jobs or always read 1.</figcaption>
</figure>

- **Bit 4, EAR**, is the beeper: the bit your program switches on and off to make a sound.
- **Bit 3, MIC**, is the tape output: the bit your program switches to save data to tape. You hear it
  too, more quietly.
- **Bits 2–0** are the border colour.

Bits 7–5 of a write are ignored. A reset clears the port: EAR and MIC start low, so a machine that has
just been switched on is silent.

Why two sound bits? Because the 48K had two outputs with two jobs, sharing one circuit. Its ULA has a
single pin for the tape sockets and the speaker: bits 4 and 3 drive it through resistors of different
values, and the signal from the EAR socket arrives on the same pin. That is why MIC is quieter than EAR,
and why what a program writes to bits 4 and 3 shows up when it reads the port back (more on that in What
port `$FE` bit 6 reads). The Next has no such pin: everything is logic inside its FPGA. It keeps the two
bits, their loudness and the read-back rules anyway, so that the programs that relied on them still work.

Reading port `$FE` returns the keyboard in bits 4–0 (a 0 for each key held in the rows the address
selects), **bit 6, the tape input**, and 1 in bits 7 and 5. With no key held and nothing on bit 6, a
read gives `$BF`.

## EAR and MIC in the mixer

The mixer adds each bit as a fixed level, the same on the left and the right side:

- **EAR** adds **512** while port `$FE` bit 4 is 1, and 0 while it is 0.
- **MIC** adds **128** while the MIC line is 1, and 0 while it is 0. The MIC line is bit 3 combined
  with the tape input (see Saving to tape); with no tape playing it is just bit 3.

The two bits together give four levels, 0, 128, 512 and 640. Drawn on the mixer's whole range, they turn
out to be a small part of it:

<figure>
{/* fig:levels */}
<svg viewBox="0 0 700 318" role="img" aria-label="Two scales. Bottom, that stretch enlarged to 0 to 700, with the four levels the two bits can make drawn as bars to scale: neither bit 0, MIC alone 128, EAR alone 512, both 640." style={{ width: "100%", height: "auto" }}>

<figcaption>EAR and MIC in the mixer, drawn to scale. Top: the four levels the two bits can make, against the sum's whole range of one side. Bottom: the same four levels, enlarged.</figcaption>
</figure>

EAR is four times as loud as MIC, the ratio the 48K's two resistors gave them. Small on that scale, yes,
but don't mistake the beeper for a quiet source: its 512 is twice what one AY channel at full volume adds
(255), and about a quarter of the DACs' largest swing on one side. Nor does it rest in the middle, as the
DACs do. Its silence is 0, so the first time a program sets bit 4, the sum jumps by 512, and the speaker
clicks, just as it did on the 48K (the mixer chapter's
[Silence is 1,536](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-audio/mixer/#silence-is-1536)
explains why the other sources do not click).

## A note from a loop

The beeper holds whatever level it was given last. That's all it does. To play a note, your program
switches bit 4 on and off at the note's pitch, and it has to do the switching itself, at exactly the right
moments. The usual way is a loop that counts the time between two writes in T-states. Here is the loop
behind the overview's beeper capture:

```z80klive
        di                  ; no interrupts: they would stretch the loop
        ld c,$fe
        ld a,$10            ; bit 4 set: EAR on
Loop:   out (c),a           ; 12 T-states   write EAR
        xor $10             ;  7            the next write's EAR is the opposite
        ld b,133            ;  7
Dly:    djnz Dly            ; 13 × 132 + 8  wait
        jp Loop             ; 10
```

How it works:

1. `out (c),a` writes the EAR bit; the speaker's cone moves.
2. `xor $10` flips bit 4 of A, so the next write is the opposite level.
3. `djnz` counts B down from 133. Each round that jumps back costs 13 T-states and the last one 8, so the
   wait is 13 × 132 + 8 = 1,724 T-states.
4. One pass of the loop is 12 + 7 + 7 + 1,724 + 10 = **1,760 T-states**: half a period of the note,
   since it takes two passes to go up and come back down.
5. The loop closes with `jp Loop` rather than `jr Loop`. A `jr` would be a byte shorter (2 bytes
   against 3) but slower: it takes 12 T-states where `jp` takes 10. In a timing loop the speed is what
   counts, and every T-state is part of the note: with `jr`, a pass would be 1,762 T-states and the note
   993 Hz.

At 3.5 MHz, 1,760 T-states take 1,760 ÷ 3,500,000 = 503 µs. A whole period is twice that, so the note is
3,500,000 ÷ (2 × 1,760) = **994 Hz**:

<figure>
{/* fig:note */}
<svg viewBox="0 0 700 330" role="img" aria-label="Four milliseconds of the loop's note, captured from the machine: a square wave of 994 Hz, 512 units from bottom to top, centred on zero. Each high and each low half is one pass of the loop, 1,760 T-states or 503 microseconds; a whole period, two passes, is 1,006 microseconds." style={{ width: "100%", height: "auto" }}>

{/* /fig:note */}
<figcaption>The loop's note, captured from the machine: a square wave whose halves are each one pass of the loop, 1,760 T-states. Klive's mix centres the wave on zero, as the capacitor after the jack does.</figcaption>
</figure>

The loop's length is the only thing that sets the pitch, and it changes in steps of 13 T-states, one round
of `djnz`. B = 132 gives 1,747 T-states and 1,002 Hz, B = 134 gives 1,773 and 987 Hz. So your program
cannot hit every pitch exactly, and a beeper tune is slightly out of tune wherever its notes fall between
those steps. A loop with a finer step (another instruction of 4 T-states in the wait, for example) gets
closer.

## The CPU's speed and contention

Because the pitch is a count of T-states, it is only as steady as the CPU's clock. Two things change how
long the same loop takes:

- **The CPU's speed.** The Next's CPU runs at 3.5, 7, 14 or 28 MHz, set by the **CPU Speed register,
  NextReg `$07`**. A T-state at 7 MHz lasts half as long, so the same loop plays twice as high: 1,989 Hz,
  an octave up. A 48K beeper tune played with the CPU at a higher speed is too high and too fast, like a
  record on the wrong speed.
- **Contention.** At 3.5 MHz, the Next stops the CPU for a few T-states when it reads memory from
  `$4000` to `$7FFF` while the screen is being drawn, as the 48K's ULA did (see
  [Contention](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/spectrum-display/#contention)). A loop that runs in that
  memory gets longer while the beam is on a line of the picture, and its note drops while it is. The
  **Peripheral 3 Setting register, NextReg `$08`**, bit 6 switches contention off, and at 7 MHz and above
  there is none.

Here is the same loop three ways:

<figcaption>The same loop captured three times, 18 ms each. At 7 MHz every pass takes half as long and the note is an octave higher. Placed in contended memory at 3.5 MHz with contention on, the loop plays 994 Hz in the border and is stretched to about 872 Hz while the picture's lines are drawn (shaded).</figcaption>
</figure>

Contention does not lower the note; it **breaks it in two**. It happens only while the beam is on one of
the picture's 192 lines, not in the border above and below, so the loop runs at two speeds in every frame:

- In the border, about 8 ms of each 20 ms frame, a pass takes its 1,760 T-states, and the note is 994 Hz.
- On the picture's lines, about 12 ms of each frame, the CPU is held up again and again, and in this capture a
  pass takes about 2,007 T-states: 872 Hz.

So the pitch drops and comes back 50 times a second, and what the ear hears is a rough, warbling tone, not a
lower note. Counting every period of a frame and dividing by 20 ms would give about 918 Hz, but that is only
arithmetic: no pass of the loop ever plays it.

How much a pass is stretched depends on the loop, not only on contention. Each of its memory accesses is held
up by up to 6 T-states, depending on where it falls in the ULA's repeating pattern of 8 T-states, and a pass
does not fall on the pattern the same way each time. Another loop, or the same loop at another address, is
stretched by a different amount; the 2,007 T-states are this loop's. What every such loop shares is the
shape: full speed in the border, slower on the picture's lines.

This is why beeper music on the Spectrum usually runs from `$8000` and up, and with interrupts off: an
interrupt would take the CPU away from the loop every 20 ms and leave a gap in the note. My advice for your
own beeper code is the same: keep it out of contended memory, and keep the interrupts off while it plays.

## Two voices from one bit

A square wave is one voice. Can you write two square waves at once to one bit? Not simply: when one voice
wants the bit high and the other wants it low, there is no level in between to give. The trick that
beeper music uses instead is to make each voice a train of **short pulses**, one at the start of each of
its periods, and to keep the bit low the rest of the time. Pulses this short rarely need the bit at the
same moment, so the bit can carry both trains, and a train of pulses at a voice's pitch sounds at that
pitch just as a square wave does. Notice how little this asks of the hardware: nothing at all. It is all
in the program.

This program plays two voices. Every pass of its loop gives each voice one fixed **slot** of time, and
in its slot a voice either switches EAR on or leaves it off. Each voice keeps an 8-bit counter, its
**phase**, and adds its **step** to it on every pass; when the addition carries out of 8 bits, the voice
has finished a period and takes its slot with EAR on:

```z80klive
        ld hl,0             ; H, L: the two voices' phases
        ld de,$0405         ; D, E: their steps, 4 and 5
Loop:   ld a,h              ;  4
        add a,d             ;  4    voice 1's phase + its step
        ld h,a              ;  4
        sbc a,a             ;  4    $FF if that carried, 0 if not
        and $10             ;  7    ... so A is EAR on or off
        out ($fe),a         ; 11    voice 1's slot begins
        ld a,l              ;  4
        add a,e             ;  4    the same for voice 2
        ld l,a              ;  4
        sbc a,a             ;  4
        and $10             ;  7
        out ($fe),a         ; 11    voice 2's slot begins
        ld a,0              ;  7
        nop                 ;  4    four nops: voice 2's slot is
        nop                 ;  4    as long as voice 1's
        nop                 ;  4
        nop                 ;  4
        out ($fe),a         ; 11    both slots over: EAR off
        jp Loop             ; 10
```

How it works:

1. Every instruction always takes the same time, whatever the phases hold: `sbc a,a` turns the carry
   into a mask instead of a jump. One pass is always **112 T-states**, so the loop runs
   3,500,000 ÷ 112 = 31,250 times a second.
2. Voice 1's slot runs from its `out` to voice 2's `out`, 34 T-states (23 for the five instructions in
   between and 11 for the `out` itself). Voice 2's slot runs from its `out` to the last one, also 34. The
   remaining 44 T-states of the pass, from the last `out` round to the first, EAR is off.
3. Voice 1's phase gains 4 each pass, so it carries once every 256 ÷ 4 = 64 passes: its pitch is
   31,250 × 4 ÷ 256 = **488.3 Hz**. Voice 2's gains 5, and carries every 51 or 52 passes: on average
   31,250 × 5 ÷ 256 = **610.4 Hz**. The two notes are a major third apart, a ratio of exactly 5 to 4.
4. The slots of a pass follow each other and never overlap, so the bit is exactly the two pulse trains
   added together.

Here is the bit over 8 ms, worked out from the program, which is the same arithmetic the CPU does:

<figure>
{/* fig:twovoice */}

The pulses are far too short to hear one by one; what your ear hears is how often they come. A frequency
spectrum of the captured sound, which shows how strong each pitch in it is, finds both notes:

<figcaption>The spectrum of the two-voice program's sound, captured from the machine: how strong each frequency from 100 to 1,300 Hz is in it. The two notes stand out at 488 and 610 Hz, each with its overtone at twice its pitch.</figcaption>
</figure>

Each note comes with **overtones**, pitches at whole multiples of it (977 and 1,221 Hz are the second
ones). A train of pulses this short has overtones almost as strong as the note itself, many of them, up
far beyond this figure; they give the beeper's chords their buzzing, nasal colour. What lies
between the notes is close to nothing. Voice 2's small unevenness, a period of 51 passes here and 52
there, spreads its peak a little but does not move it.

## What two voices cost

Well, nothing comes for free, and a chord from one bit is no exception:

- **Loudness.** A voice is on for 34 of every 7,168 T-states (voice 1's period of 64 passes), less than
  half a percent of the time. Short pulses are quiet: the louder a voice should be, the longer its slot,
  so a program sets the **volume of a voice by the width of its pulses**. Two voices need two slots that
  never meet, so each can have at most half the pass.
- **Pitch.** A voice's pitch is the loop's rate times its step divided by 256. Finer pitches need a
  16-bit phase, which costs more T-states per pass and lowers the rate.
- **The CPU.** While the tune plays, the CPU does nothing else: every T-state of the loop is counted.
  This is why beeper music on the 48K played on title screens and between levels, and why the 128K's AY
  chip, which plays by itself, was such a step forward.

## Saving to tape

A program saves to tape by switching **port `$FE` bit 3**, MIC, in a pattern of pulses that encodes its
data. The **MIC socket** of the Next carries that pattern to a tape recorder, as the 48K's did.

The MIC line is not bit 3 alone, though. It is bit 3 combined with the tape input (see Loading from tape)
and with the EAR bit that the Raspberry Pi's audio link can produce ([the chapter on audio from the
Raspberry Pi](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-audio/pi-audio/#loading-a-tape-from-the-pi)) by an **exclusive or**: the line is 1 when an odd number of the three are 1.
The same line feeds both the MIC socket and the mixer's MIC input:

<figure>
{/* fig:input */}
<svg viewBox="0 0 700 548" role="img" aria-label="A diagram of the beeper's lines. Top: the EAR socket feeds the input stage, noise rejection then relaxation, whose output is the tape input. Right: the Pi's EAR bit. Port $FE bit 6 is an or of three things: the tape input exclusive-or the Pi's EAR bit; port $FE bit 4, EAR; and port $FE bit 3, MIC, and-ed with the issue 2 keyboard switch, NextReg $08 bit 0. Bottom: the MIC line is port $FE bit 3 exclusive-or the tape input exclusive-or the Pi's EAR bit; it goes to the MIC socket and into the mixer at 128 units." style={{ width: "100%", height: "auto" }}>

<figcaption>The beeper's lines. Port $FE bit 3 is combined with the tape input and the Pi's EAR bit into the MIC line, which goes to the MIC socket and into the mixer. The EAR socket's signal passes the input stage and becomes the tape input, which port $FE bit 6 reads together with the EAR and MIC bits.</figcaption>
</figure>

Why an exclusive or? Because it keeps each signal whole. With no tape playing, the MIC line is bit 3,
exactly what your program writes. While a tape plays and the program holds bit 3 still, the line follows
the tape: if bit 3 is 1, upside down, which sounds the same and carries the same edges. What follows from
that:

- **A loading tape is heard**, at MIC's level of 128: the screech of a loading program comes out of the
  speaker, as it did on the 48K.
- **The MIC socket echoes the tape input**: a recorder plugged into it while another plays into EAR
  records what the Next hears.
- **A program saving data** switches bit 3 while no tape plays, and the socket carries its pulses
  unchanged.

## Loading from tape

A tape recorder plays its signal into the **EAR socket**. What the Next reads from it is not the raw
voltage but a clean level, 0 or 1, produced by an **input stage** in two steps, noise rejection and
relaxation, both counted on the master clock.

A tape's signal is analogue and noisy: near an edge it may cross the threshold several times in a row. The
first step, **noise rejection**, passes a new level on only when it has held steady for **two ticks of the
1.75 MHz clock**: any change starts the count again. Depending on where the ticks fall, that takes between
0.6 and 1.14 µs, so a pulse shorter than 0.6 µs never reaches the CPU and one longer than 1.14 µs always
does. A real edge arrives about a microsecond late, which no loader notices.

## Relaxation: a level left at 1

The second step deals with a tape that stops on a 1. Some recordings, and some tape circuits, leave the
level at 1 after the last pulse instead of returning to 0, and a loader waiting for the next edge would see
the bit stuck. The original machines did not have that problem: the 48K's input relaxed back to 0 after
at most about 800 µs, and the ZX81 expects it to within about 1,300 µs. The Next's **relaxation** step
copies them: a 1 that lasts 64 ticks of a 54,688 Hz clock (512 master clocks each), between 1.15 and
1.17 ms depending on where the ticks fall, turns into a 0, until the input goes low and high again.

Here are the two steps, worked out from the rules on made-up input signals:

<figcaption>The input stage, worked out from its rules. Top, over 6 µs: a half-microsecond glitch is rejected, and a real edge passes about a microsecond late. Bottom, over 5 ms: a tone passes untouched, and a level left at 1 relaxes to 0 after about 1.17 ms.</figcaption>
</figure>

The relaxation cuts only highs longer than about 1.15 ms, so a tone with half-periods shorter than
that, above about 430 Hz, passes untouched. A Spectrum tape is far quicker: its pilot tone's pulses are
2,168 T-states long, 619 µs, and its data pulses shorter still. The result of both steps is the **tape
input**, the level that port `$FE` bit 6 reads and that the MIC line carries.

## What port `$FE` bit 6 reads

Bit 6 is not the tape input alone. It is 1 when any of these is true at the moment of the read:

1. **The tape input**, combined by exclusive or with the Pi's EAR bit, is 1.
2. **Port `$FE` bit 4**, EAR, was last written as 1.
3. **Port `$FE` bit 3**, MIC, was last written as 1, and the **issue 2 keyboard** is on: the Peripheral 3
   Setting register, NextReg `$08`, bit 0 (0 after power-on).

So a loader must keep EAR at 0 while it reads the tape, or bit 6 reads 1 whatever the tape plays; the
48K ROM's loader does, and keeps MIC at 1. And bit 6 follows the bits at once: the read straight after a
write that clears bit 4 already returns 0. On a 48K, the shared pin you met in Two bits of sound is an
analogue line that takes a moment to fall, so a read that comes very soon after the write can still find a
1; the Next's bit 6 is logic, and has no such delay.

## The issue 2 keyboard

The 48K was built in several board revisions, called issues. On the early **issue 2** boards, writing a 1
to the MIC bit was enough to make bit 6 read 1; from **issue 3** on, only the EAR bit did. Software written
on an issue 2 machine sometimes compared the whole byte read from port `$FE` against a fixed value, with
bit 6 assumed to be 1, instead of testing the key bits alone. On a later machine bit 6 read 0, the
comparison failed, and the program saw keys that were not pressed, or ignored the ones that were. Haunted
keyboards, and all because of one bit nobody meant to test.

The Next behaves as issue 3 after power-on, as most Spectrums did. NextReg `$08` bit 0 gives such a program
the issue 2 machine it was written for: with it set, the MIC bit reads back in bit 6 too.

Let me warn you, though: the setting has a price. The 48K ROM's loader holds MIC at 1 the whole time it
listens, so with the issue 2 keyboard on, bit 6 reads 1 throughout, the loader never sees an edge, and
loading from tape fails. A program that needs the setting should switch it on after it has loaded, not
before.

## The internal speaker

The Next has a small speaker on its board, which plays the left and right sums added together. The
Peripheral 3 Setting register, NextReg `$08`, bit 4 switches it on (1, the value after power-on) or off.

The **Peripheral 2 Setting register, NextReg `$06`**, bit 6 sends the beeper **only to the internal
speaker**. With it set, and the speaker on, EAR and MIC leave the mix that reaches the audio jack and HDMI,
and the speaker plays them alone:

<figcaption>Where the beeper goes. Left, as it is after power-on: EAR and MIC are added into the mix with every other source, and the jack, HDMI and the speaker all play it. </figure>

What is this good for? Keeping two kinds of sound apart: key clicks, alerts and the tape's loading noise
come from the machine itself, while the music plays in your headphones or on the television undisturbed.
The beeper is moved, not copied: the headphones no longer hear it. On the speaker, EAR and MIC keep their
loudness and their 4-to-1 ratio; the mixer chapter's
[The internal speaker](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-audio/mixer/#the-internal-speaker)
shows the bits of the speaker's word.

## How Klive does it

Klive's Next core is a C program compiled to WebAssembly; the beeper, the port and the tape input are
three parts of it.

**The beeper.** Every write to port `$FE` that changes EAR or the MIC line is recorded with the moment it
happened, to the T-state. When the mixer needs a sample, the beeper works out what share of the sample's
time each of the four levels lasted, so an edge that falls in the middle of a sample gives it a level in
between, as the smoothing after the real converter would. For the HDMI output (Machine › Sound › Output) it
takes the level at the sample's moment instead, as HDMI does (the mixer chapter's
[HDMI audio](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-audio/mixer/#hdmi-audio)).

The mixer adds EAR as 512 units and MIC as 128, and passes them through a filter that removes their
constant level slowly, as the capacitor after the real jack does; that is why every capture here moves
around zero. With NextReg `$06` bit 6 set and the speaker on, EAR and MIC are left out of the jack's and
HDMI's mix and play on the internal speaker output alone.

**Port `$FE`.** A write stores EAR, MIC and the border colour; a read builds bit 6 from the tape input, the
Pi's EAR bit, the stored EAR bit and, with NextReg `$08` bit 0 set, the stored MIC bit, at the moment of the
read. There is no analogue delay: an emulator of the 48K would model the slow fall of its shared pin, but
the Next has none.

**The tape input.** Klive's tape deck plays a `.tap` or `.tzx` file into the Next's EAR socket (see
[Tapes](/en/guides/media/tapes/)). Its level goes through the input stage described in Loading from tape,
counted exactly as the hardware counts it, one tick of the 1.75 MHz clock at a time; the stage is brought
up to date only when something looks at it (an instruction, a read of port `$FE`, a sample of sound), and
skips ahead while nothing plays. The result feeds port `$FE` bit 6 and the MIC line, so a loading tape is
heard as on the real machine. The deck also loads quickly, by taking over the 48K ROM's loading routine
when the program calls it, and records what the program saves through MIC into a new tape file.

And if you want to read the code yourself, here is where it lives:

| What | Where |
|---|---|
| EAR, MIC and the MIC line, the recorded changes, a sample's share of each level | `src/emu/machines/zxNext/wasm/zxnext/zxnext-beeper.c` |
| Port `$FE` writes and reads, bit 6 | `zxnext-ula.c` in the same folder |
| The tape deck and the input stage (noise rejection, relaxation) | `zxnext-tape.c` in the same folder |
| The levels, the beeper's filter, the speaker-only switch | `zxnext-audio-mixer.c` in the same folder |

## What to take home

Let's look back at the two bits. The beeper is two bits of **port `$FE`**, which any even port address reaches:
**bit 4, EAR**, the beeper itself, and **bit 3, MIC**, the tape output, beside the border colour in bits
2–0. A reset clears them. In the mixer, EAR adds **512** and MIC **128** to both sides, 4 to 1 as on the
48K, and because their silence is 0, the first switch-on clicks.

A note is a **timing loop**: two passes make one period, so 1,760 T-states a pass at 3.5 MHz is
**994 Hz**, and pitches come in steps of the loop's smallest change. The **CPU's speed** (NextReg `$07`)
scales the pitch. **Contention** (3.5 MHz only, switched by NextReg `$08` bit 6) slows the loop only while
the picture's lines are drawn, so the note does not drop to one lower pitch: it plays at two speeds,
dropping and coming back 50 times a second.

**Two voices** share the bit as trains of **short pulses** in slots that never meet. A voice's pitch is
how often its pulses come, its loudness their width, and the CPU does nothing else while they play.

On the tape side, the **MIC line** is bit 3 exclusive-or the tape input and the Pi's EAR bit; it feeds the
**MIC socket** and the mixer, so a loading tape is heard and echoed. The **EAR socket** passes an **input
stage** that rejects pulses shorter than about **a microsecond** and relaxes a 1 held for about
**1.15–1.17 ms** to 0. **Bit 6** reads that tape input (exclusive-or the Pi's EAR bit), or the EAR bit, or,
with the **issue 2 keyboard** (NextReg `$08` bit 0), the MIC bit, and follows them at once.

Finally, NextReg `$08` bit 4 switches the **internal speaker** on, and NextReg `$06` bit 6 moves the beeper
to the speaker alone. Klive records each change with its T-state, mixes each sample's share of every level,
filters the constant level away as the jack's capacitor does, and runs the tape through the same input
stage. 28:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["Why two sound bits? Because the 48K had two outputs with two jobs, sharing one circuit. Its ULA has a\nsingle pin for the tape sockets and the speaker: bits 4 and 3 drive it through resistors of different\nvalues, and the signal from the EAR socket arrives on the same pin. That is why MIC is quieter than EAR,\nand why what a program writes to bits 4 and 3 shows up when it reads the port back (more on that in What\nport ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"$$FE"}]," bit 6 reads). The Next has no such pin: everything is logic inside its FPGA. It keeps the two\nbits, their loudness and the read-back rules anyway, so that the programs that relied on them still work."]}]
29:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["Reading port ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"$$FE"}]," returns the keyboard in bits 4–0 (a 0 for each key held in the rows the address\nselects), ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"bit 6, the tape input"}],", and 1 in bits 7 and 5. With no key held and nothing on bit 6, a\nread gives ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"$$BF"}],"."]}]

2f:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["EAR is four times as loud as MIC, the ratio the 48K’s two resistors gave them. Small on that scale, yes,\nbut don’t mistake the beeper for a quiet source: its 512 is twice what one AY channel at full volume adds\n(255), and about a quarter of the DACs’ largest swing on one side. Nor does it rest in the middle, as the\nDACs do. Its silence is 0, so the first time a program sets bit 4, the sum jumps by 512, and the speaker\nclicks, just as it did on the 48K (the mixer chapter’s\n",["$","$L20",null,{"href":"/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-audio/mixer/#silence-is-1536","className":"x:focus-visible:nextra-focus x:text-primary-600 x:underline x:hover:no-underline x:decoration-from-font x:[text-underline-position:from-font]","children":"Silence is 1,536"}],"\nexplains why the other sources do not click)."]}]

37:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["The loop’s length is the only thing that sets the pitch, and it changes in steps of 13 T-states, one round\nof ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"djnz"}],". A T-state at 7 MHz lasts half as long, so the same loop plays twice as high: 1,989 Hz,\nan octave up. 40:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":"How much a pass is stretched depends on the loop, not only on contention. Each of its memory accesses is held\nup by up to 6 T-states, depending on where it falls in the ULA’s repeating pattern of 8 T-states, and a pass\ndoes not fall on the pattern the same way each time. Another loop, or the same loop at another address, is\nstretched by a different amount; the 2,007 T-states are this loop’s. What every such loop shares is the\nshape: full speed in the border, slower on the picture’s lines."}]
41:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["This is why beeper music on the Spectrum usually runs from ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"$$8000"}]," and up, and with interrupts off: an\ninterrupt would take the CPU away from the loop every 20 ms and leave a gap in the note. My advice for your\nown beeper code is the same: keep it out of contended memory, and keep the interrupts off while it plays."]}]
42:["$","h2",null,{"id":"two-voices-from-one-bit","className":"x:tracking-tight x:text-slate-900 x:dark:text-slate-100 x:font-semibold x:target:animate-[fade-in_1.5s] x:mt-10 x:border-b x:pb-1 x:text-3xl nextra-border","children":["Two voices from one bit",["$","$L21",null,{"id":"two-voices-from-one-bit"}]]}]
43:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["A square wave is one voice. Can you write two square waves at once to one bit? Not simply: when one voice\nwants the bit high and the other wants it low, there is no level in between to give. The trick that\nbeeper music uses instead is to make each voice a train of ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"short pulses"}],", one at the start of each of\nits periods, and to keep the bit low the rest of the time. Pulses this short rarely need the bit at the\nsame moment, so the bit can carry both trains, and a train of pulses at a voice’s pitch sounds at that\npitch just as a square wave does. Notice how little this asks of the hardware: nothing at all. It is all\nin the program."]}]
44:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["This program plays two voices. Every pass of its loop gives each voice one fixed ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"slot"}]," of time, and\nin its slot a voice either switches EAR on or leaves it off. Each voice keeps an 8-bit counter, its\n",["$","strong",null,{"children":"phase"}],", and adds its ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"step"}]," to it on every pass; when the addition carries out of 8 bits, the voice\nhas finished a period and takes its slot with EAR on:"]}]

58:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["A tape’s signal is analogue and noisy: near an edge it may cross the threshold several times in a row. The\nfirst step, ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"noise rejection"}],", passes a new level on only when it has held steady for ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"two ticks of the\n1.75 MHz clock"}],": any change starts the count again. Depending on where the ticks fall, that takes between\n0.6 and 1.14 µs, so a pulse shorter than 0.6 µs never reaches the CPU and one longer than 1.14 µs always\ndoes. A real edge arrives about a microsecond late, which no loader notices."]}]

5a:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["The second step deals with a tape that stops on a 1. Some recordings, and some tape circuits, leave the\nlevel at 1 after the last pulse instead of returning to 0, and a loader waiting for the next edge would see\nthe bit stuck. The original machines did not have that problem: the 48K’s input relaxed back to 0 after\nat most about 800 µs, and the ZX81 expects it to within about 1,300 µs. The Next’s ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"relaxation"}]," step\ncopies them: a 1 that lasts 64 ticks of a 54,688 Hz clock (512 master clocks each), between 1.15 and\n1.17 ms depending on where the ticks fall, turns into a 0, until the input goes low and high again."]}]

61:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":"So a loader must keep EAR at 0 while it reads the tape, or bit 6 reads 1 whatever the tape plays; the\n48K ROM’s loader does, and keeps MIC at 1. And bit 6 follows the bits at once: the read straight after a\nwrite that clears bit 4 already returns 0. On a 48K, the shared pin you met in Two bits of sound is an\nanalogue line that takes a moment to fall, so a read that comes very soon after the write can still find a\n1; the Next’s bit 6 is logic, and has no such delay."}]

63:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["The 48K was built in several board revisions, called issues. On the early ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"issue 2"}]," boards, writing a 1\nto the MIC bit was enough to make bit 6 read 1; from ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"issue 3"}]," on, only the EAR bit did. Software written\non an issue 2 machine sometimes compared the whole byte read from port ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"$$FE"}]," against a fixed value, with\nbit 6 assumed to be 1, instead of testing the key bits alone. On a later machine bit 6 read 0, the\ncomparison failed, and the program saw keys that were not pressed, or ignored the ones that were. Haunted\nkeyboards, and all because of one bit nobody meant to test."]}]
64:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["The Next behaves as issue 3 after power-on, as most Spectrums did. A program that needs the setting should switch it on after it has loaded, not\nbefore."}]

6d:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"The beeper."}]," Every write to port ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"$$FE"}]," that changes EAR or the MIC line is recorded with the moment it\nhappened, to the T-state. When the mixer needs a sample, the beeper works out what share of the sample’s\ntime each of the four levels lasted, so an edge that falls in the middle of a sample gives it a level in\nbetween, as the smoothing after the real converter would. 6e:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["The mixer adds EAR as 512 units and MIC as 128, and passes them through a filter that removes their\nconstant level slowly, as the capacitor after the real jack does; that is why every capture here moves\naround zero. With NextReg ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"$$06"}]," bit 6 set and the speaker on, EAR and MIC are left out of the jack’s and\nHDMI’s mix and play on the internal speaker output alone."]}]
6f:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":["Port ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"$$FE"}],"."]}]," A write stores EAR, MIC and the border colour; a read builds bit 6 from the tape input, the\nPi’s EAR bit, the stored EAR bit and, with NextReg ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"$$08"}]," bit 0 set, the stored MIC bit, at the moment of the\nread. There is no analogue delay: an emulator of the 48K would model the slow fall of its shared pin, but\nthe Next has none."]}]
70:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"The tape input."}]," Klive’s tape deck plays a ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":".tap"}]," or ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":".tzx"}]," file into the Next’s EAR socket (see\n",["$","$L20",null,{"href":"/en/guides/media/tapes/","className":"x:focus-visible:nextra-focus x:text-primary-600 x:underline x:hover:no-underline x:decoration-from-font x:[text-underline-position:from-font]","children":"Tapes"}],"). Its level goes through the input stage described in Loading from tape,\ncounted exactly as the hardware counts it, one tick of the 1.75 MHz clock at a time; the stage is brought\nup to date only when something looks at it (an instruction, a read of port ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"$$FE"}],", a sample of sound), and\nskips ahead while nothing plays. The result feeds port ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"$$FE"}]," bit 6 and the MIC line, so a loading tape is\nheard as on the real machine. The deck also loads quickly, by taking over the 48K ROM’s loading routine\nwhen the program calls it, and records what the program saves through MIC into a new tape file."]}]

74:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["Let’s look back at the two bits. The beeper is two bits of ",["$","strong",null,{"children":["port ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"$$FE"}]]}],", which any even port address reaches:\n",["$","strong",null,{"children":"bit 4, EAR"}],", the beeper itself, and ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"bit 3, MIC"}],", the tape output, beside the border colour in bits\n2–0. 75:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["A note is a ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"timing loop"}],": two passes make one period, so 1,760 T-states a pass at 3.5 MHz is\n",["$","strong",null,{"children":"994 Hz"}],", and pitches come in steps of the loop’s smallest change. The ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"CPU’s speed"}]," (NextReg ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"$$07"}],")\nscales the pitch. ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"Contention"}]," (3.5 MHz only, switched by NextReg ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"$$08"}]," bit 6) slows the loop only while\nthe picture’s lines are drawn, so the note does not drop to one lower pitch: it plays at two speeds,\ndropping and coming back 50 times a second."]}]
76:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Two voices"}]," share the bit as trains of ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"short pulses"}]," in slots that never meet. A voice’s pitch is\nhow often its pulses come, its loudness their width, and the CPU does nothing else while they play."]}]
77:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["On the tape side, the ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"MIC line"}]," is bit 3 exclusive-or the tape input and the Pi’s EAR bit; it feeds the\n",["$","strong",null,{"children":"MIC socket"}]," and the mixer, so a loading tape is heard and echoed. The ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"EAR socket"}]," passes an ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"input\nstage"}]," that rejects pulses shorter than about ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"a microsecond"}]," and relaxes a 1 held for about\n",["$","strong",null,{"children":"1.15–1.17 ms"}]," to 0. ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"Bit 6"}]," reads that tape input (exclusive-or the Pi’s EAR bit), or the EAR bit, or,\nwith the ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"issue 2 keyboard"}]," (NextReg ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"$$08"}]," bit 0), the MIC bit, and follows them at once."]}]
78:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["Finally, NextReg ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"$$08"}]," bit 4 switches the ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"internal speaker"}]," on, and NextReg ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"$$06"}]," bit 6 moves the beeper\nto the speaker alone.
